II. 
Overall Revenue Requirement 
2 
Q. 
Is the Company proposing any changes to the revenue requirement in 
3 
this filing? 
4 
A. 
Yes.  The Company’s corrections and updates filing reflected a revenue 
5 
requirement of $195.6 million.  Based on the Company’s review of the 
6 
direct testimony filed by Staff and the intervenors, as well as discovery 
7 
submitted since the time of the corrections and updates filing, the Company 
8 
has accepted several adjustments and reflected certain updates to the 
9 
revenue requirement.  Exhibit ___ (RRP-1R) sets forth the Company’s 
10 
position on each of the adjustments proposed by Staff.   
11 
 
12 
In addition, the revenue requirement reflected in the corrections and updates 
13 
filing assumed the NESE project would be completed by the winter of 
14 
2020/2021.  In this rebuttal filing, the Company adjusted the revenue 
15 
requirement to assume the NESE project will not be completed within that 
16 
timeframe.  Specifically, the Company includes adjustments that were 
17 
identified in its supplemental filing, which the Staff Revenue Requirements 
18 
Panel (“SRRP”) reflected in Exhibit ___ (SRRP-2), Schedule 7a, together 
19 
with certain other adjustments (e.g., an updated revenue forecast and 
20 
updates/corrections discussed by the Company’s GIOP).  In principle, the 
21 


--- Page 7 ---
Case 19-G-0309 
 
Rebuttal Testimony of the Revenue Requirements Panel 
 
Page 5 of 71 
Company agrees with the need for the additional no-NESE project 
1 
adjustments reflected by the SRRP in Exhibit ___ (SRRP-2), Schedule 7b, 
2 
but disagrees with the calculation of some of those adjustments.  In addition, 
3 
the Company disagrees with the adjustment to Other Initiatives listed in that 
4 
schedule, as discussed by the Future of Heat Panel.  The Company included 
5 
the agreed adjustments in the revenue requirement.  Any disagreement is 
6 
discussed below.   
7 
 
8 
Q. 
What is the updated Rate Year revenue requirement? 
9 
A. 
The updated revenue requirement is $190.491 million.    
10 
 
11 
Q. 
Does the Panel have any general comments regarding the adjustments 
12 
proposed by Staff to the revenue requirement? 
13 
A. 
Yes.  It is important for the Commission to recognize that, in total, the 
14 
adjustments and proposals made by Staff, if adopted, would provide a level 
15 
of rate relief that would not provide the Company with the revenues needed 
16 
to provide safe and reliable service to customers, earn a reasonable return 
17 
on its investment, continue to attract capital on commercially reasonable 
18 
terms, and deliver on the Commission and State’s clean energy goals.  Staff 
19 
proposes a return on equity of 8.2 percent, which would be the lowest ROE 
20 
approved for a gas distribution utility in the United States.  Moreover, 
21 


--- Page 8 ---
Case 19-G-0309 
 
Rebuttal Testimony of the Revenue Requirements Panel 
 
Page 6 of 71 
although Staff says throughout its testimony that the Company should invest 
1 
at levels the Company deems appropriate, Staff’s proposals effectively deny 
2 
the Company funding for programs and investments it believes are critical 
3 
to advancing safety and reliability.  Indeed, in many cases, Staff’s proposals 
4 
would require the Company to absorb material costs of doing business.  
5 
Specific examples include: 
6 
• Information Technology.  A disallowance of $114.4 million of the 
7 
Company’s projected capital IT investments that must be incurred 
8 
to modernize the Company’s aging infrastructure and enable key 
9 
customer and operations programs.   
10 
• GBE Program.  A disallowance of more than $57 million in costs 
11 
that will need to be incurred for the benefit of customers to progress 
12 
the project, which is needed to build a modern platform that 
13 
enhances safety and supports customer demands. 
14 
• Unidentified Savings.  An adjustment to impute more than $9 
15 
million in incremental, unidentified savings on top of the more than 
16 
$36 million in savings already reflected in the Rate Year. 
17 
• Newtown Creek.  An adjustment that effectively requires the 
18 
Company to fund a portion of the costs of the Newtown Creek 
19 
project notwithstanding the agreement in the Joint Proposal in the 
20 


--- Page 9 ---
Case 19-G-0309 
 
Rebuttal Testimony of the Revenue Requirements Panel 
 
Page 7 of 71 
2016 KEDNY and KEDLI Rate Cases that the Company would 
1 
proceed with the project. 
2 
Future of Heat.  Virtually no funding for the Company’s Future of 
3 
Heat programs, which are designed to advance cost-effective, 
4 
reliable, heating sources that help reduce carbon emissions on the 
5 
gas network. 
6 
In addition to proposing significant disallowances of costs that the 
7 
Company will need to incur in the Rate Year, Staff also proposes a series of 
8 
downward-only true-ups for costs associated with full-time equivalents, 
9 
Service Company IT rent expense, paving, GBE, and credit cards.  At the 
10 
same time, Staff rejects the limited true-ups sought by the Company for 
11 
costs over which it has limited control such as property taxes and CSC 
12 
O&M spending.  The proposed downward only true-ups effectively 
13 
preclude the Company from retaining efficiencies found to offset the costs 
14 
that Staff proposes to disallow and thus achieve the additional productivity 
15 
that Staff proposes to impute.  On top of this, Staff proposes to modify the 
16 
Company’s performance metrics substantially, adding more stringent 
17 
targets, but not providing funding to meet these new targets.  These 
18 
proposals effectively result in a further disallowance of costs by increasing 
19 
the likelihood that the Company will incur negative revenue adjustments.   
20 
 
21 


--- Page 10 ---
Case 19-G-0309 
 
Rebuttal Testimony of the Revenue Requirements Panel 
 
Page 8 of 71 
The combined effect of all of Staff’s adjustments is that the Company would 
1 
be saddled with the lowest ROE authorized in recent years for a gas utility 
2 
and, at the same time, effectively precluded from earning it.  This end-result 
3 
is unreasonable and unjustified and creates grave concern as to whether the 
4 
Company will be able to continue to attract necessary capital on 
5 
commercially reasonable terms in an environment in which the Company is 
6 
facing increased opposition to any sales growth in its business and is 
7 
experiencing a supply constraint that, along with Staff’s proposals in this 
8 
filing, is effectively foreclosing it from pursuing current growth 
9 
opportunities.     
10 
 
11 
Over the term of its current rate plan, the Company has invested billions of 
12 
dollars in its gas distribution network to support safe operations, met and 
13 
exceeded all customer and gas performance metrics, and improved its 
14 
compliance performance year over year.  In that same time period, the 
15 
regulatory landscape has seen significant changes that will likely have 
16 
material impacts on the Company’s costs and programs going forward.  The 
17 
proposed rate plan was designed to address in a balanced manner issues 
18 
raised by Staff and other stakeholders and take important step changes 
19 
necessary to lead the transition to New York’s energy future.  As discussed 
20 
herein, as well as through the Company’s direct and rebuttal testimonies, 
21 


--- Page 11 ---
Case 19-G-0309 
 
Rebuttal Testimony of the Revenue Requirements Panel 
 
Page 9 of 71 
the recommendations advanced by Staff in this proceeding would not 
1 
achieve a similar result.

III. 
Operating Revenues Adjustments 
4 
Q. 
Please summarize Staff’s proposed adjustments to Operating 
5 
Revenues.   
6 
A. 
Staff proposed three adjustments to Operating Revenues that are reflected 
7 
in Exhibit __ (SRRP-2), Schedules 7a, 7b, and 7c.  The first adjustment was 
8 
made to reduce Operating Revenues to reflect the unavailability of the 
9 
NESE project.  The second adjustment was made to reflect the Company’s 
10 
latest sales forecast.  Although the Company agrees these adjustments are 
11 
required, the Company does not agree with Staff’s calculations of the 
12 
adjustments.  The rebuttal testimony of the Company’s Rate Design Panel 
13 
provides the correct calculations.  The third adjustment reflects the Staff 
14 
Policy Panel’s (“SPP”) recommendation to impute $3.281 million in 
15 
revenues associated with the Newtown Creek project. 
16 
 
 
17 


--- Page 12 ---
Case 19-G-0309 
 
Rebuttal Testimony of the Revenue Requirements Panel 
 
Page 10 of 71 
Q. 
Does the Company agree with the adjustment related to the Newtown 
1 
Creek project? 
2 
A. 
No.  As discussed in the rebuttal testimony of the Company’s Future of Heat 
3 
Panel, the Company disagrees with this adjustment. 
4 
 
5 
IV. 
O&M Expense Adjustments 
6 
Q. 
Please summarize Staff’s proposed adjustments to O&M expense.   
7 
A. 
Staff’s proposed adjustments to O&M expense are shown in Exhibit ___ 
8 
(SRRP-2), Schedules 7a, 7b, and 7c.     
9 
 
10 
 
A. 
Consultants 
11 
Q. 
Does Staff make any adjustments to consultants expense? 
12 
A. 
Yes.  The SRRP proposes to disallow $1.146 million in costs associated 
13 
with work performed by a consultant, AlixPartners LLP, in the Historic Test 
14 
Year.  The basis for the adjustment is the SRRP’s belief that these charges 
15 
will not recur in the Rate Year.   
16 
 
17 
Q. 
Does the Company agree with Staff’s proposed disallowance? 
18 
A. 
No.  As explained in the response to IR DPS-806, the Company uses 
19 
consultants like AlixPartners LLP to provide services aimed at ensuring the 
20 
efficient delivery of the IT portfolio.  While the SRRP contends that “it is 
21 


--- Page 13 ---
Case 19-G-0309 
 
Rebuttal Testimony of the Revenue Requirements Panel 
 
Page 11 of 71 
not reasonable” for the Company to use a consultant to perform this type of 
1 
work again in the Rate Year (at 15-16), the Company submits that it would 
2 
be unreasonable and imprudent if it did not.  The IT portfolio is ever-
3 
changing to keep pace with evolving customer and stakeholder needs.  The 
4 
Company will continue to engage the services of consultants like 
5 
AlixPartners LLP to look proactively for ways to maintain existing 
6 
efficiencies and ensure the effective operation of the portfolio.  Such work 
7 
would be expected of any major company with an IT portfolio as large as 
8 
the Company’s – especially given the potential benefits that could accrue to 
9 
customers from this work.  Therefore, these costs are fully expected to recur 
10 
in the Rate Year and should not be adjusted from the cost of service. 
11 
 
12 
Q. 
In support of its position, the SRRP contends (at 16-17) that individual 
13 
work performed by AlixPartners LLP such as work to refresh the 
14 
Company’s IT strategy will not recur because the Company has 
15 
already undertaken this work.  Does the Company agree with the 
16 
SRRP’s position?  
17 
A. 
No.  The SRRP’s view of what constitutes recurring work is too narrowly 
18 
focused.  This is just one item under a broad category of work that is done 
19 
to manage the IT portfolio.  Although this specific item may not recur every 
20 
year, that does not mean that other work to evaluate and assess the IT 
21 


--- Page 14 ---
Case 19-G-0309 
 
Rebuttal Testimony of the Revenue Requirements Panel 
 
Page 12 of 71 
portfolio will not recur.  For instance, work to assist with contract 
1 
negotiations, work to analyze system investments, and benchmarking with 
2 
other organizations is all work that consultants such as AlixPartners LLP 
3 
provide and will continue to provide in the Rate Year.    
4 
 
5 
Q. 
Is there an additional reason why the SRRP’s adjustment should be 
6 
rejected? 
7 
A. 
Yes.  As explained in the response to IRs DPS-931 and DPS-933, the work 
8 
performed by AlixPartners LLP resulted in annual run rate savings of $22.1 
9 
million, of which $16.6 million is reflected in the revenue requirement in 
10 
the Rate Year and Data Years.  Therefore, customers received a significant 
11 
benefit from the very work for which the the SRRP seeks to disallow cost 
12 
recovery.  This further shows the unreasonableness of the SRRP’s proposed 
13 
adjustment.   
14 
 
15 
 
B. 
Contractors 
16 
Q. 
Did Staff propose any adjustments to contractors expense? 
17 
A. 
Yes.  The SRRP proposed two adjustments to contractors expense. 
18 
 
 
19 


--- Page 15 ---
Case 19-G-0309 
 
Rebuttal Testimony of the Revenue Requirements Panel 
 
Page 13 of 71 
Q. 
Please explain the first adjustment. 
1 
A. 
The first adjustment reflects a correction to contractors expense associated 
2 
with Local Law 30 that the Company inadvertently did not include in 
3 
corrections and updates.  The adjustment reduces contractors expense by 
4 
$5.201 million. 
5 
 
6 
Q. 
Does the Company agree with this adjustment? 
7 
A. 
Yes. 
8 
 
9 
Q. 
Please discuss the second adjustment. 
10 
A. 
The SRRP proposes an adjustment to snow removal costs based on the use 
11 
of a three-year average, which it asserts (at 22) “will smooth out the year to 
12 
year changes” and “produce a more reasonable Rate Year forecast.”  The 
13 
adjustment reduces contractors expense by $1.263 million. 
14 
 
15 
Q. 
Does the Company agree with this adjustment? 
16 
A. 
No.  The Company believes that use of the Historic Test Year to forecast 
17 
snow removal costs is reasonable and appropriate.  Two main reasons 
18 
support the Company’s position.   
19 
 
20 


--- Page 16 ---
Case 19-G-0309 
 
Rebuttal Testimony of the Revenue Requirements Panel 
 
Page 14 of 71 
First, use of the Historic Test Year treats customers and the Company fairly 
1 
over time.  This expense has been forecast the same way in several past 
2 
National Grid rate cases, and Staff has never proposed using a different 
3 
methodology.  In KEDNY’s last rate case, the rate allowance for snow 
4 
removal expense was approximately $0.689 million.  Actual expenses in 
5 
calendar years 2017 and 2018 (rate years 1 and 2 of the current rate plan) 
6 
were $1.8 million and $3.337 million, respectively.  Calendar year 2019 
7 
(rate year three) expense to date is $2.115 million with certain winter 
8 
months still forthcoming.  Utilizing the SRRP’s proposed three-year 
9 
average in this case is inconsistent with the historic forecasting 
10 
methodology and there is no reason to believe that it will result in a more 
11 
accurate forecast of Rate Year expense.     
12 
 
13 
Q. 
What is the second reason for why the SRRP’s proposal is 
14 
unreasonable? 
15 
A. 
Use of a consistent forecasting methodology helps minimize controversy by 
16 
avoiding situations such as the one here where parties pick and choose 
17 
different methodologies depending on the end-result.  Notably, the SRRP is 
18 
not proposing to use a three-year average to calculate KEDLI’s snow 
19 
removal expense, but instead is using the Historic Test Year expense as the 
20 


--- Page 17 ---
Case 19-G-0309 
 
Rebuttal Testimony of the Revenue Requirements Panel 
 
Page 15 of 71 
basis for the Rate Year forecast.  For all these reasons, the SRRP’s 
1 
adjustment should be rejected.      
2 
 
3 
C. 
Other Expense 
4 
Q. 
Did Staff propose an adjustment to other expense? 
5 
A. 
Yes.  The Staff Accounting Panel (“SAP”) proposes a 25 percent reduction 
6 
to the Company’s Historic Test Year costs associated with violations and 
7 
fines incurred for work performed in New York City.   
8 
 
9 
Q. 
What is the basis for the SAP’s proposal?   
10 
A. 
The SAP contends (at 18) that a 25 percent cost reduction is a “conservative 
11 
approach” to reflect process improvements undertaken by the Company to 
12 
reduce the number of violations and fines and to account for violations and 
13 
fines the SAP believes result from circumstances under the Company’s 
14 
control.  This adjustment reduces the revenue requirement by $0.920 
15 
million. 
16 
 
17 
Q. 
Did the SAP provide any calculation or prepare any analysis showing 
18 
that a 25 percent reduction in violations and fines is in fact achievable? 
19 
A. 
No.  The SAP simply claimed (at 18-19) that it is “reasonable to assume the 
20 
benefits” associated with the Company’s existing process improvements 
21 


--- Page 18 ---
Case 19-G-0309 
 
Rebuttal Testimony of the Revenue Requirements Panel 
 
Page 16 of 71 
will “come to fruition prior to the end of the Rate Year” without providing 
1 
any support for how the additional benefits could be achieved.   
2 
 
3 
Q. 
Does the Company agree with the SAP’s position?  
4 
A. 
No.  The SAP’s position completely disregards the realities of performing 
5 
work in New York City.  As the Company explained in the response to IR 
6 
DPS-550, these costs are incurred in the ordinary course of business as the 
7 
Company must regularly excavate in streets and sidewalks in one of the 
8 
largest and most densely populated metropolitan areas in the country.  
9 
While the Company takes precautions to avoid violations and fines, given 
10 
the nature of many of the Company’s projects, these costs are not cost-
11 
effectively avoidable above the level of reductions that the Company has 
12 
already achieved.  As explained in the response to IR DPS-550, these 
13 
violations are often the result of unanticipated field conditions (e.g., 
14 
subsurface facilities, parking conditions, heavy traffic) that require the 
15 
Company to work in a larger area or for a longer period than was 
16 
contemplated at the time the permit was secured.  The Company could take 
17 
additional measures solely for purposes of avoiding violations, such as 
18 
repeated demobilizations and remobilizations to avoid working past permit 
19 
times, but such measures would not be as efficient and would result in more 
20 
customer disruptions.  Thus, while the Company will continue its efforts to 
21 


--- Page 19 ---
Case 19-G-0309 
 
Rebuttal Testimony of the Revenue Requirements Panel 
 
Page 17 of 71 
avoid violations and fines to the extent possible, it believes that these 
1 
charges are a reasonable and unavoidable cost of doing business in its 
2 
service territory and should be fully recoverable from customers.   
3 
 
4 
Q. 
Please respond to the SAP’s assertion that the Company’s process 
5 
improvements will result in a 25 percent reduction in violations and 
6 
fines. 
7 
A. 
As described in the SAP’s testimony (at 14-17), the Company has 
8 
implemented numerous process improvements to mitigate this cost.  What 
9 
the SAP fails to recognize, however, is that these process improvements 
10 
have kept these costs from increasing even higher and that the Historic Test 
11 
Year reflects the reasonably expected results from these efforts.  A 25 
12 
percent improvement beyond these efforts is simply unreasonable.  
13 
Specifically, achieving the reductions to date was difficult and many 
14 
resulted from initiatives that could be easily implemented at the time but are 
15 
not repeatable.  Additionally, even with KEDNY’s efforts to obtain 
16 
reductions in this area, the expectations of the New York City Department 
17 
of Transportation (“DOT”), which continues to expand its code 
18 
enforcement resources and capabilities, are fluid and evolving such that new 
19 
areas triggering fines and violations could emerge.  For example, KEDNY 
20 
recently has experienced increased scrutiny on issues related to traffic flow 
21 


--- Page 20 ---
Case 19-G-0309 
 
Rebuttal Testimony of the Revenue Requirements Panel 
 
Page 18 of 71 
and pedestrian accessibility – areas that have not been a major focus in the 
1 
past and which are resulting in more fines despite the Company’s prudent 
2 
efforts.  For instance, the Company’s procedures require that, for leak 
3 
investigations, excavations be kept open to vent trapped gas readings to 
4 
make the area safe before permanent repairs can be made.  This requires the 
5 
Company to barricade the excavation to avoid pedestrian injury.  The DOT, 
6 
however, requires that the full width of the sidewalk be kept open outside 
7 
of the time stated on the permit, thus bringing the Company’s work into 
8 
conflict with DOT requirements.    
9 
 
10 
Q. 
In proposing its adjustment, did the SAP address the impact that the 
11 
Company’s increased capital plan will have on this cost element? 
12 
A. 
No, they did not.  While the Company strives to react to changing 
13 
expectations and continuously improve in these areas, the issue is further 
14 
compounded by the significant expansion of the Company’s work plan, 
15 
including increased CSC work and LPP targets, above Historic Test Year 
16 
levels.  As the Company explained in the response to IR DPS-928, an 
17 
increase in the amount of permitted work will likely result in more fines.  
18 
While KEDNY will continue its ongoing efforts to reduce the number of 
19 
fines and violations received and work with the DOT where possible, the 
20 
costs are ultimately an unavoidable cost of doing business in New York City 
21

and are not expected to significantly decrease in the coming years.

Q.

1 
A. 
No.  As indicated in the rebuttal testimony of Company Witness Heaphy, 
2 
the Company does not agree with Mr. Gadomski’s testimony on this issue 
3 
and does not accept the proposed adjustment.     
4 
 
5 
Q. 
Please explain Staff’s second adjustment to labor expense.   
6 
A. 
The SRRP proposes (at 24-29) imputing a vacancy rate to the Company’s 
7 
labor forecast of KEDNY employees based on their assumption that the 
8 
Company’s forecast does not account for vacancies.  The SRRP developed 
9 
their vacancy rate of 3.16 percent by dividing the number of unfilled 
10 
positions within KEDNY by the targeted employee level for that company.  
11 
The SRRP’s adjustment was specific to FTEs who directly charge time to 
12 
KEDNY and did not include Service Company employees whose time is 
13 
allocated to KEDNY.  The adjustment reduces the Company’s Rate Year 
14 
labor expense forecast by an additional $2.759 million.   
15 
 
16 
Q. 
Does the Company agree with the SRRP’s application of a vacancy rate 
17 
to the labor expense forecast? 
18 
A. 
No.  To develop the labor forecast, the Company used adjusted headcounts 
19 
for KEDNY and the Service Company as of December 31, 2018 (the end of 
20 
the Historic Test Year).  The December 31st headcounts only included 
21 


--- Page 26 ---
Case 19-G-0309 
 
Rebuttal Testimony of the Revenue Requirements Panel 
 
Page 24 of 71 
positions actually filled by employees as of that date.  It did not include 
1 
positions that were open or vacant at that time, even though such positions 
2 
existed.   
3 
 
4 
Table 1 below depicts open positions (those positions approved to be filled) 
5 
and positions pending approval at calendar-year end 2018 for all National 
6 
Grid companies, including KEDNY and employees that may allocate or 
7 
directly charge time to the Company.  None of these vacancies were 
8 
included in the Company’s labor forecast.  Therefore, the SRRP’s 
9 
assumption that the Company’s labor forecast does not account for 
10 
vacancies is incorrect.  Indeed, the Company’s forecast does assume that 
11 
vacancies as of December 31, 2018 will continue to occur in the Rate Year 
12 
just in a different manner than the SRRP proposes.  Rather than applying a 
13 
separate vacancy rate to the forecast, the Company entirely removed any 
14 
actual vacancies from the labor forecast.  In doing so, the Company bears 
15 
the financial risk of vacancies that were not included in the headcount but 
16 
may be filled after the end of the Historic Test Year.  As such, application 
17 
of a vacancy rate to the previously reduced labor expense forecast is a 
18 
double count of vacant positions. 
19

F. 
Transportation Expense 
4 
Q. 
Please describe the SRRP’s proposed adjustment to the Company’s 
5 
forecast of transportation expense. 
6 
A. 
The SRRP proposes (at 42) a downward adjustment of $0.619 million to the 
7 
lease component of transportation expense.  The adjustment is based on the 
8 
SRRP’s belief that the Rate Year costs are “excessive” because the 
9 
Company forecasts replacing 180 more vehicles from the end of the Historic 
10 
Test Year through the end of the Rate Year than it had during the prior three-
11 
year period.   
12 
 
13 
Q. 
Does the Company agree with the SRRP’s adjustment? 
14 
A. 
No.  The Company’s forecast of replacement vehicles is based on a detailed 
15 
fleet plan that carefully considers the type of vehicle, the age of the vehicle, 
16 
maintenance history, and work for which the vehicle is utilized to develop 
17 
a projected replacement date for each vehicle.   
18 
 
19 
In KEDNY’s last rate proceeding, Staff proposed an adjustment of $0.732 
20 
million in transportation lease expense, which was reflected in the Joint 
21 


--- Page 31 ---
Case 19-G-0309 
 
Rebuttal Testimony of the Revenue Requirements Panel 
 
Page 29 of 71 
Proposal adopted by the Commission.  To manage its vehicle replacements 
1 
within this rate allowance, the Company modified its vehicle replacement 
2 
schedule to stretch out the lifecycle of certain vehicles.  This adjustment 
3 
increased the average age of vehicles that are needed to provide service to 
4 
the Company’s customers and help maintain and operate the gas network. 
5 
The Company estimates that the replacement of approximately 88 vehicles, 
6 
calculated by dividing the $0.723 million lease expense adjustment from the 
7 
previous rate case by the $8,205 average per vehicle cost calculated by Staff 
8 
in Exhibit ___ (SRRP-4), were delayed by this adjustment and therefore 
9 
included in the forecast of replacements in this case. 
10 
 
11 
 
12 
Q. 
The SRRP asserts (at 39) that because the Company has in the past 
13 
utilized vehicles with expired lease dates it is “inappropriate” to assume 
14 
that vehicles with expiring leases will be replaced in the Rate Year.  Is 
15 
this a fair assumption? 
16 
A. 
No.  The lease end date is based on the expected lifecycle of each vehicle.  
17 
While the timing of replacements varies depending on the specific 
18 
conditions of the vehicles (e.g., depending on the performance or usage of 
19 
the vehicle, the asset life may fall short of or exceed the planned lifecycle), 
20 


--- Page 32 ---
Case 19-G-0309 
 
Rebuttal Testimony of the Revenue Requirements Panel 
 
Page 30 of 71 
in general each vehicle with a lease term that has expired should be replaced 
1 
to ensure reliable fleet operations.   
2 
 
3 
While the Company was able to increase the average age of its fleet over 
4 
the past three years to stay within the budgeted rate allowance without 
5 
unduly compromising reliability, this cannot continue indefinitely if the 
6 
Company hopes to maintain a reliable vehicle fleet.  The Company needs to 
7 
replace more vehicles than it has in the past three years to restore the 
8 
reliability of the vehicle fleet to its optimal level.  The Company does not 
9 
believe that deviating from the planned lifecycle replacement is reasonable 
10 
or in the best interests of customers, as it will lead to higher maintenance 
11 
costs and potentially compromise the integrity of the Company’s vehicle 
12 
fleet.  Moreover, the SRRP has submitted no evidence that rebuts or 
13 
contradicts the reasonableness of the Company’s replacement schedule, and 
14 
instead unreasonably substitutes its own judgement in place of the 
15 
Company’s fleet plan.  For these reasons, the SRRP’s adjustment should be 
16 
rejected. 
17

A. 
Yes.  The Staff SIR Panel (at 29) recommends a downward adjustment of 
4 
$15.168 million.  The rebuttal testimony of Company Witness Chuck 
5

Q.

10

14 
to demonstrate that the Company requires an incentive to carry out SIR 
15 
activities in a cost effective manner, which is a prerequisite for requiring 
16 
shareholders to absorb SIR costs.  Indeed, KEDNY’s cost control efforts are 
17 
well documented, follow the Commission’s best practices inventory set 
18 
forth in the SIR Generic Order, and have been reviewed by Staff in this and 
19 
other proceedings and found to be reasonable.  PULP conducted no 
20 
discovery on the Company’s cost mitigation strategies and procurement 
21 


--- Page 40 ---
Case 19-G-0309

Page 38 of 71 
practices and offered no testimony to suggest the Company’s practices were 
1 
inadequate or imprudent.  In fact, PULP’s only argument, a blanket 
2 
statement that shareholders should, in equity, bear a portion of these costs 
3 
was rejected by the Commission in the SIR Generic Order and, most 
4 
recently, by the Commission in the 2016 KEDNY and KEDLI rate case 
5 
when PULP made the same argument.   
6 
 
7 
 
K. 
Savings 
8 
Q. 
What level of savings is typically reflected in a rate filing? 
9 
A. 
Rate cases traditionally include a productivity adjustment on the premise 
10 
that well-run companies should be able to find a modest measure of 
11 
efficiencies in the rate year.  The adjustment is typically calculated as one 
12 
percent of total labor expense and payroll taxes in recognition of how 
13 
difficult it is to achieve savings year after year.  In this case, the traditional 
14 
productivity adjustment would result in efficiencies totaling $3.894 million.  
15 
The Company, however, has proposed to provide customers with more than 
16 
$36 million in savings in the Rate Year – a level more than eight times the 
17 
traditional productivity adjustment.  The SRRP asserts that $9.475 million 
18 
of additional efficiencies should be imputed above this level.  If the SRRP’s 
19 
position were adopted, this would equate to savings of more than 11 times 
20 


--- Page 41 ---
Case 19-G-0309 
 
Rebuttal Testimony of the Revenue Requirements Panel 
 
Page 39 of 71 
the traditional productivity adjustment, which is simply unrealistic and not 
1 
supported.   
2 
 
3 
Q. 
Will the level of savings reflected by the Company in the rate filing be 
4 
difficult to achieve? 
5 
A. 
Yes.  It is a challenge for any business to achieve a one percent efficiency 
6 
reduction each year let alone eight times that number.  In its filing, the 
7 
Company committed to reduce its costs through an ambitious effort (the 
8 
Accelerate Program), despite that many of the initiatives to achieve these 
9 
reductions are difficult and potentially not sustainable.  Indeed, the 
10 
Company is working tirelessly to reduce its costs to mitigate bill impacts 
11 
for customers while continuing to invest to assure reliability and advance 
12 
our systems for the future.   
13 
 
14 
To reduce costs to the magnitude the Company proposes in this filing 
15 
requires major changes to people, processes, and systems.  This requires 
16 
time and effort.  There is a major risk that the savings will not be realized 
17 
because competing priorities will distract from the Company’s ability to 
18 
deliver the initiatives.  For example, new work, changes in law, new 
19 
regulatory requirements, changes in customer expectations, or major 
20 
weather events could all impact the Company’s ability to progress 
21 


--- Page 42 ---
Case 19-G-0309 
 
Rebuttal Testimony of the Revenue Requirements Panel 
 
Page 40 of 71 
initiatives or deliver savings.  Staff’s filing further exacerbates this risk 
1 
through their proposals to disallow costs that the Company will need to 
2 
incur in the Rate Year to ensure the delivery of safe and reliable service.   
3 
 
4 
Q. 
The SRRP asserts that savings associated with Implementation Level 
5 
(“IL”) 3 Accelerate Program initiatives should be imputed into the 
6 
revenue requirement, contending that savings from these initiatives 
7 
“have been identified and quantified,” and been defined by the 
8 
Company as “bankable” (at 56-57).  Does the Panel agree? 
9 
A. 
No.  While IL3 Accelerate Program initiatives have been assigned an initial 
10 
estimate of potential savings, the estimate is aspirational as the business has 
11 
not taken any of the steps that are necessary to implement the initiative.  In 
12 
IL3, the business develops a plan to implement an initiative.  Until each step 
13 
in the implementation plan has been completed, whether the initiative can 
14 
be delivered and the savings ultimately achieved is unknown.  As the 
15 
Company explained in the response to IR DPS-755, “bankable” means that 
16 
the Company is willing to invest the time and resources into pursuing the 
17 
initiative, as the Company has limited resources to dedicate to 
18 
implementing initiatives.  This does not mean that the initiative can actually 
19 
deliver savings, however.  There are risks that this initial estimate, which is 
20 
really a placeholder to prioritize initiatives, may never materialize or may 
21 


--- Page 43 ---
Case 19-G-0309 
 
Rebuttal Testimony of the Revenue Requirements Panel 
 
Page 41 of 71 
change materially.  These risks include changes in assumptions as the 
1 
initiative progresses, delays in implementation, or cancelation of the 
2 
initiative because, despite the Company’s efforts, the implementation plan 
3 
could not be delivered (e.g., a vendor may not be willing to provide a 
4 
volume discount or a new contract to reduce costs could not be negotiated).  
5 
Therefore, contrary to the SRRP’s position, IL3 estimates are not quantified 
6 
let alone known and measurable, which is the standard for inclusion in the 
7 
revenue requirement.  Rather, IL3 estimates are speculative and 
8 
unidentified efficiency initiatives that are properly subsumed within the 
9 
traditional productivity adjustment.   
10 
 
11 
 
In the Panel’s direct testimony, we explained the rigorous process 
12 
undertaken to progress initiatives.  Each initiative must pass through 
13 
implementation levels ranging from IL0 (purely an idea) to IL5 (fully 
14 
implemented), where they are scrutinized by groups from the Company’s 
15 
Finance, Regulation and Pricing, and Business Units, among others.  It is 
16 
not until IL4 where an initiative undergoes implementation.  In IL4 the 
17 
business has completed the steps in its implementation plan that are a 
18 
prerequisite to achieving benefits.  The SRRP’s proposal ignores this 
19 
process that was implemented to provide transparency to the business and 
20 


--- Page 44 ---
Case 19-G-0309 
 
Rebuttal Testimony of the Revenue Requirements Panel 
 
Page 42 of 71 
its regulators of the full amount of savings that were achieved, and the 
1 
simple fact that initiatives in IL3 are entirely uncertain.  
2 
 
3 
Q. 
The SRRP further asserts (at 58) that initiatives “move quickly through 
4 
implementation levels, meaning initiatives currently at Level Three will 
5 
soon be at Levels Four or Five, and thus, savings will likely accrue 
6 
during the Rate Year.”  The SRRP also claims (at 57) that few IL3 
7

8

A. 
As explained in the response to IR DPS-755, initially, initiatives moved 
10 
quickly through the various implementation levels because they were 
11 
already in flight at the beginning of the Accelerate Program.  However, that 
12 
movement occurred during the earlier stages of the program.  Now that the 
13 
program is past the inaugural period, the movement of initiatives has 
14 
slowed.   
15 
 
16 
In addition, while the Company agrees that certain initiatives in IL3 can 
17 
move to ILs 4 and 5 quickly, that movement depends on whether the 
18 
initiative can be easily implemented.  With respect to the initiatives 
19 
currently in IL3 for which the SRRP is proposing to impute the associated 
20 
savings to the Company, on average, these initiatives have been in IL3 for 
21

more than 150 days – more than five times the 26 day average an initiative

that can be easily implemented typically moves from IL3 to IL4.  Given the 
2

3 
these initiatives will progress to implementation and deliver the aspirational

Q. 
Please summarize the Company’s position concerning the SRRP’s 
1 
proposed imputation of the IL3 initiatives.   
2 
A. 
There is no reasonable basis for the Commission to adopt the SRRP’s 
3

4 
IL3 initiatives may move to IL4 and IL5.  This is purely speculative, 
5 
however, and the SRRP has provided no rationale basis for including 
6 
unknown savings in the revenue requirement.  The Company, at all times 
7

8 
Company explained in the response to IR DPS-916, while many initiatives 
9 
could arguably have been removed as one-time in nature, the Company 
10 
included all initiatives in IL4 and IL5 to mitigate customer bill impacts.  The 
11 
$32.483 million in Accelerate Program savings plus the $3.894 million in 
12 
productivity to capture the IL3 initiatives is reasonable and should be 
13 
adopted.  The Company should not be penalized by including preliminary 
14 
savings estimates from initiatives that have not yet been delivered – and 
15 
may never be.  Rather, the Companies should be encouraged to be 
16 
innovative and test concepts without the fear that preliminary and 
17 
aspirational estimates will be held against them.  The savings adjustments 
18 
proposed by the SRRP would have a chilling effect on future savings 
19 
programs that benefit customers.

VI. 
Federal Income Taxes 
4 
Q. 
How did the Company propose to reflect the amortization of the annual 
5 
Excess Deferred Income Taxes (“EDIT”) for the unprotected plant and 
6 
non-plant balances resulting from the Tax Cuts and Jobs Act in the rate 
7 
filing? 
8 
A. 
The Company proposed to amortize the balances over 44 years in 
9 
accordance with the manner in which protected EDIT was amortized under 
10 
the Average Rate Assumption Method (“ARAM”) required by the Internal 
11 
Revenue Service normalization rules.  
12 
 
13 
Q. 
What is Staff’s position on this issue? 
14 
A. 
The SAP (at 46) rejects the use of the ARAM and instead proposes a 10-
15 
year amortization period for the unprotected plant and non-plant balances.  
16 
The proposal is premised upon that use a shorter period will more rapidly 
17 
reduce the revenue requirement and return the tax benefits to customers 
18 
quicker rather than any analysis.    
19 
 
20 


--- Page 55 ---
Case 19-G-0309 
 
Rebuttal Testimony of the Revenue Requirements Panel 
 
Page 53 of 71 
Q. 
Does the Company agree with the SAP’s use of a 10-year amortization 
1 
period? 
2 
A. 
No.  The Company believes the amortization period should match the 
3 
service life of the asset.  If the unprotected EDIT is returned to customers 
4 
over 10-years, as proposed by the SAP, there would be a mismatch for over 
5 
30 years in which customers would receive benefits before the Company 
6 
receives the cash benefits.  Moreover, if the SAP’s proposal is adopted, 
7 
future changes to tax rates could result in the Company having to collect 
8 
money from customers that it previously returned.  For these reasons, the 
9 
Company submits that its proposal to align the amortization life with the 
10 
reversal of the temporary differences is more equitable and in the best 
11 
interests of customers.

rebuttal testimonies of the GIOP, IT, Shared Services, and Future of Heat 
18 
Panels and Company Witness Christopher Connolly. 
19 
 
 
20 


--- Page 58 ---
Case 19-G-0309 
 
Rebuttal Testimony of the Revenue Requirements Panel 
 
Page 56 of 71 
Q. 
Does Staff recommend any new deferral mechanisms? 
1 
A. 
Yes.  In addition to the downward-only new hire and paving expense 
2 
deferral discussed above, various Staff witnesses recommend a number of 
3 
new deferrals.  The rebuttal testimony of the Company’s GIOP, IT, Shared 
4 
Services, and Future of Heat Panels and Company Witness Christopher 
5 
Connolly respond to Staff’s proposals.   
6 
 
7 
 
C. 
Earnings Sharing Mechanism 
8 
 
9 
Q. 
The Staff Policy Panel (“SPP”) recommends (at 12-14) that the 
10 
Commission establish an Earnings Sharing Mechanism (“ESM”) for 
11 
the Companies for each 12-month period following the Rate Year.  Does 
12 
the Panel agree with this recommendation? 
13 
A. 
No, we do not.  ESMs are typically a feature of multi-year rate plans that 
14 
are submitted to the Commission as joint proposals that reflect the broad 
15 
agreement of most, if not all, parties to Commission rate proceedings.  It is 
16 
our understanding that the Commission only recently imposed an ESM in a 
17 
litigated one-year rate proceeding.  From our perspective, imposing ESMs 
18 
in litigated cases would be poor regulatory policy in that it would discourage 
19 
the Companies from finding ways to manage their businesses in a manner 
20 
that minimizes the need for time consuming and expensive rate 
21 
proceedings.  Moreover, the SPP’s ESM recommendation essentially 
22 


--- Page 59 ---
Case 19-G-0309 
 
Rebuttal Testimony of the Revenue Requirements Panel 
 
Page 57 of 71 
requests the Commission to impose a mechanism that would confiscate a 
1 
portion of the Companies’ earnings without making a finding as to what a 
2 
just and reasonable ROE would be in years beyond the Rate Year or 
3 
conducting the process that is typically followed in establishing utility rates. 
4 
 
5 
Q. 
Please explain. 
6 
A. 
In this case, the Commission is setting rates that it will determine to be just 
7 
and reasonable for a Rate Year beginning April 1, 2020 and ending March 
8 
31, 2021.  Among the decisions being made is the appropriate ROE that 
9 
should be applied to the Company’s rate base during this period.  As the 
10 
Commission is aware, the just and reasonable ROE changes over time.  
11 
However, if this case is litigated, the Commission will make no finding as 
12 
to what the appropriate ROE for the Company will be for any period beyond 
13 
the Rate Year.  At the same time, however, under the SPP’s ESM 
14 
mechanism in years beyond the Rate Year, the Company could be required 
15 
to forfeit a portion of its earnings regardless of whether those earnings 
16 
would, depending on capital market conditions at the time, be no greater 
17 
than the amount needed to enable the Company to earn a compensatory 
18 
ROE.  Such a result would be procedurally defective and substantively 
19 
confiscatory. 
20 
 
 
21 


--- Page 60 ---
Case 19-G-0309 
 
Rebuttal Testimony of the Revenue Requirements Panel 
 
Page 58 of 71 
 
The Commission has the ability under the Public Service Law to monitor 
1 
the Company’s earnings and take appropriate action to the extent that it 
2 
believes that the Company’s rates are producing excessive returns.  There 
3 
is no need for the Commission to impose an ESM to ensure that rates remain 
4 
just and reasonable and it is quite possible that the results of imposing such 
5 
a mechanism would not be just and reasonable.  For these reasons, the SPP’s 
6 
ESM recommendation should be rejected. 
7

Case 19-G-0309 
 
Rebuttal Testimony of the Revenue Requirements Panel 
 
Page 61 of 71 
 
B. 
Earnings Base/Capitalization/Working Capital Adjustment 
1 
Q. 
Please 
describe 
your 
understanding 
of 
the 
Earnings 
2 
Base/Capitalization 
(“EB/CAP”)/Working 
Capital 
adjustments 
3 
recommended by the SRRP. 
4 
A. 
In its filing, KEDNY proposed to make a normalization adjustment to its 
5 
proposed EB/CAP calculation and an offsetting adjustment to a separate 
6 
working capital component in the Historic Test Year to isolate and explicitly 
7 
identify a CSC accounts receivable balance of $58.744 million.  The 
8 
$58.774 million adjustment represents the outstanding balance of capital 
9 
and operating expenditures that the City of New York (“NYC”) is required 
10 
to reimburse KEDNY for capital expenditures that KEDNY has incurred to 
11 
enable NYC to execute its municipal work plan.  The outstanding balance 
12 
results from the fact that there is a considerable period of time between 
13 
when KEDNY incurs reimbursable CSC costs and when those costs are 
14 
reimbursed by NYC. 
15 
 
 
16 
In addition to the Historic Test Year CSC adjustment, KEDNY proposed to 
17 
make a CSC-related working capital adjustment to reflect the projected 
18 
increase in working capital associated with reimbursable CSC expenditures 
19 
between the end of the Historic Test Year and the end of the Rate Year.  
20 
This adjustment increased Rate Year rate base by $28.273 million.  This 
21 


--- Page 64 ---
Case 19-G-0309 
 
Rebuttal Testimony of the Revenue Requirements Panel 
 
Page 62 of 71 
adjustment reflects the fact that the balance of reimbursable CSC costs is 
1 
projected to increase as a result of both an increase in CSC work and the 
2 
continued lag in the time it will take NYC to reimburse KEDNY for its CSC 
3 
expenditures. 
4 
 
 
5 
While the SRRP acknowledges that there is no revenue requirement impact 
6 
associated with Historic Test Year CSS adjustment of $58.744 million, the 
7 
SRRP nonetheless opposes this adjustment as well as the proposed Rate 
8 
Year adjustment.   The SRRP’s recommendation would reduce KEDNY’s 
9 
rate base by $28.273 million. 
10 
 
11 
Q. 
Do you agree with the SRRP’s recommendations concerning KEDNY’s 
12 
proposed CSC-related working capital adjustments? 
13 
A. 
No.  The proposed adjustments would unreasonably require KEDNY’s 
14 
shareholders to bear the entire cost of financing $28.273 million of CSC-
15 
related expenditures that KEDNY is required to incur to assist NYC.  These 
16 
CSC-related costs are prudently incurred by KEDNY to fulfill its 
17 
obligations to NYC.  There is no sound reason for prohibiting KEDNY from 
18 
recovering the cost of financing these reasonable expenditures during the 
19 
period in which they remain unreimbursed. 
20 
 
21 


--- Page 65 ---
Case 19-G-0309 
 
Rebuttal Testimony of the Revenue Requirements Panel 
 
Page 63 of 71 
Q. 
The SRRP (at 78) asserts that the Commission should reject KEDNY’s 
1 
proposed CSC working capital adjustment because the Commission 
2 
has a long-standing practice of relying on the Federal Energy 
3 
Regulatory Commission’s (“FERC”) formula for calculating the 
4 
amount of working capital included in rate base.  Do you agree with 
5 
this assertion? 
6 
A. 
No, for two primary reasons.  First, the FERC working capital formula 
7 
establishes a level of working capital associated with a utility’s O&M 
8 
expenses.  It provides a level of working capital equal to approximately 45-
9 
days of O&M expenses.  The reimbursable CSC amounts are not part of 
10 
KEDNY’s O&M expenses and therefore the working capital associated 
11 
with those amounts is not recovered through the application of the FERC 
12 
formula.  Second, it is our understanding that FERC itself follows a policy 
13 
of applying the 45-day formula in the absence of hardships that would 
14 
justify a departure from the established formula.  The circumstances 
15 
surrounding the reimbursable CSC expenditures would create an 
16 
unreasonable hardship for KEDNY by forcing it to forego recovery of 
17 
approximately $2.414 million of carrying charges in the Rate Year if its 
18 
proposal to include the projected amount of reimbursable Rate Year CSC 
19 
expenditures in rate base is rejected. 
20 
 
21 


--- Page 66 ---
Case 19-G-0309 
 
Rebuttal Testimony of the Revenue Requirements Panel 
 
Page 64 of 71 
Q. 
Has the Commission previously recognized that there may be situations 
1 
where the FERC working capital formula does not capture the impact 
2 
of particular costs or revenues on the need for working capital such 
3 
that an additional working capital adjustment is necessary to 
4 
compensate a utility for the costs of financing a lag in the collection of 
5 
costs or revenues? 
6 
A. 
Yes.  In an Order dated June 1, 1992 in Case 90-G-0112, the Commission 
7 
acknowledged that the recognition of unbilled revenue in the rates charged 
8 
by the Long Island Lighting Company (“LILCO”) created an additional 
9 
financing requirement for LILCO that required compensation in excess of 
10 
that provided by the FERC working capital formula.  The reimbursable CSC 
11 
expenditures for which KEDNY seeks a working capital adjustment in rate 
12 
base are analogous to the unbilled revenues that were not supported by the 
13 
FERC working capital allowance. 
14 
 
15 
Q. 
The SRRP claims (at 81) that using the FERC formula and adopting 
16 
the Company’s proposal in this case would result in a double count.  Do 
17 
you agree? 
18 
A. 
No.  On the contrary, it would result in permitting KEDNY to recover a 
19 
proper amount of working capital associated with its O&M expenses and a 
20 
proper amount of working capital associated with its reimbursable CSC 
21 


--- Page 67 ---
Case 19-G-0309 
 
Rebuttal Testimony of the Revenue Requirements Panel 
 
Page 65 of 71 
expenditures.  As discussed above, the return on unreimbursed CSC costs 
1 
is not recovered through the FERC working capital formula. 
2 
 
3 
Q. 
The SRRP claims (at 82-83) that if KEDNY’s proposed adjustment for 
4 
working 
capital 
associated 
with 
reimbursable 
CSC-related 
5 
expenditures is permitted then all items affecting working capital, such 
6 
as income taxes, would have to be analyzed from a lead-lag perspective.  
7 
Do you agree? 
8 
A. 
No.  As the SRRP notes, the Commission adopted the FERC working 
9 
capital formula in the early 1970s.  At that time, the Commission was 
10 
certainly aware that the formula explicitly captured working capital 
11 
associated with O&M expenses but did not explicitly capture working 
12 
capital associated with other non-O&M costs that were incurred at the time, 
13 
such as income taxes.  Thus, it is reasonable to conclude that the FERC 
14 
formula was intended to provide a level of working capital that would 
15 
compensate utilities for the costs they were incurring at the time the formula 
16 
was adopted.  However, KEDNY did not obtain the ability to seek 
17 
reimbursement of CSC expenditures until the passage of the New York 
18 
State Gas Facility Cost Allocation Act in 1988.  Moreover, the amount of 
19 
reimbursable CSC expenditures has grown dramatically in recent years due 
20 
to an expansion of NYC’s municipal construction program.  Thus, contrary 
21 


--- Page 68 ---
Case 19-G-0309 
 
Rebuttal Testimony of the Revenue Requirements Panel 
 
Page 66 of 71 
to SRRP’s claim, it would be completely reasonable and appropriate for the 
1 
Commission to recognize a working capital adjustment for reimbursable 
2 
CSC costs without making any other modifications to its policy of using the 
3 
FERC formula to compensate utilities for the financing of costs that were a 
4 
part of utility revenue requirements at the time the Commission adopted the 
5 
FERC working capital formula.  
6 
 
7 
Q. 
The SRRP further asserts (83-84) that the Commission should reject 
8 
KEDNY’s CSC-related working capital adjustment because it is 
9 
equivalent to making a Rate Year adjustment to the historic EB/CAP 
10 
adjustment.  Do you agree with this assertion? 
11 
A. 
No.  KEDNY is not seeking to change the Commission’s method of 
12 
determining the EB/CAP adjustment.  Under KEDNY’s proposal, the 
13 
EB/CAP adjustment will continue to apply only to the Historic Test Year.  
14 
KEDNY is instead seeking recovery of a single, material financing cost 
15 
associated with reimbursable CSC expenditures that KEDNY will 
16 
unavoidably incur during the Rate Year.  The Commission’s obligation to 
17 
set just and reasonable rates mandates that it afford KEDNY the opportunity 
18 
to recover the carrying cost associated with its Rate Year reimbursable CSC 
19 
balance. 
20 
 
21 


--- Page 69 ---
Case 19-G-0309 
 
Rebuttal Testimony of the Revenue Requirements Panel 
 
Page 67 of 71 
Q. 
The SRRP contends (at 84-89) that the Commission should reject 
1 
KEDNY’s CSC-related working capital adjustment because KEDNY 
2 
has been “at least partially, at fault for and contributed to, the long lag 
3 
time in getting reimbursed by the City for its reimbursable CSC costs.”  
4 
Do you agree? 
5 
A. 
No.  The SRRP unreasonably utilizes KEDNY’s efforts to improve the 
6 
process of billing and recovering reimbursable CSC costs from NYC as a 
7 
basis to deny KEDNY the opportunity to recover a carrying charge on 
8 
prudently incurred reimbursable CSC costs.  The primary cause of the 
9 
backlog with respect to reimbursable CSC costs is the fact that NYC 
10 
significantly increased its municipal work plan in 2017.  This in turn 
11 
increased CSC construction costs as well as requested reimbursements.  In 
12 
addition, NYC has also increased the audit scrutiny applied to reimbursable 
13 
CSC costs, further increasing the payment lag.  While KEDNY has been 
14 
working with NYC representatives to attempt to improve the timing for 
15 
processing CSC invoices, the simple fact of the matter is that NYC has 
16 
policies and procedures in place that render it likely that there will be a 21-
17 
month lag between the time reimbursable CSC expenditures are booked and 
18 
the time they are paid.  The $28.273 million rate base adjustment proposed 
19 
in this case for the Rate Year reflects KEDNY’s reasonable efforts to speed 
20 
up collection of reimbursable CSC costs.  The fact that KEDNY has 
21 


--- Page 70 ---
Case 19-G-0309 
 
Rebuttal Testimony of the Revenue Requirements Panel 
 
Page 68 of 71 
attempted to speed up the recovery process provides no basis to require 
1 
KEDNY to bear the full costs of financing unrecovered reimbursable CSC 
2 
costs. 
3 
 
4 
Q. 
The SRRP further asserts (at 89-91) that KEDNY’s proposed working 
5 
capital adjustment for CSC reimbursable expenditures should be 
6 
rejected because KEDNY’s cost sharing agreement with NYC does not 
7 
include either a deadline for payment or the ability to assess late 
8 
payment charges.  Do you agree? 
9 
A. 
No.  The Gas Facility Cost Allocation Act has no provisions that compels 
10 
NYC to reimburse the Company for CSC costs within any particular 
11 
timeframe or pay a late charge for failure to do so.  In the absence of 
12 
legislation to the contrary, KEDNY has no ability to compel NYC to modify 
13 
the cost sharing agreement to include either a late payment charge or a firm 
14 
payment deadline.  While the Company will continue to try to reach an 
15 
amended agreement with NYC, it is inconceivable that any such agreement 
16 
will not provide NYC a significant amount of time to process, audit and pay 
17 
KEDNY’s reimbursable CSC costs.  The 21-month period underlying 
18 
KEDNY’s cost recovery proposal in this case is the expected timeframe for 
19 
payments from NYC for the foreseeable future. 
20 
 
21 


--- Page 71 ---
Case 19-G-0309 
 
Rebuttal Testimony of the Revenue Requirements Panel 
 
Page 69 of 71 
Q. 
The SRRP also proposes (at 92-95) removing from rate base a $22.316 
1 
million regulatory asset for KEDNY associated with the cost sharing 
2 
account because such costs are already reflected in the EB/CAP 
3 
adjustment.  Do you agree? 
4 
A. 
No.  The regulatory asset associated with the cost sharing agreement was 
5 
included in rate base in determining the Historic Test Year EB/CAP 
6 
adjustment.  It is inconsistent for the SRRP to remove the regulatory asset 
7 
from the forecast but not from the EB/CAP adjustment.  The SRRP 
8 
acknowledged that this should be the case in its response to IR NG-19, 
9 
which is included in Exhibit (RRP-5R).  Making the appropriate adjustment 
10 
to the EB/CAP calculation eliminates any revenue requirement impact of 
11 
Staff’s proposed adjustments. 
12 
 
13 
Q. 
The SRRP asserts (at 96) that KEDNY’s accounting practices with 
14 
respect to reimbursable CSC expenditures could cause (i) plant in 
15 
service balances to be overstated, and (ii) the unreasonable addition of 
16 
interest onto the costs of reimbursable CSC capital projects due to the 
17 
application of an Allowance for Funds Used During Construction 
18 
(“AFUDC”).  Do you have any comments concerning this assertion? 
19 
A. 
Yes.  As the Panel previously discussed, there is no merit in the SRRP’s 
20 
speculation that KEDNY’s practices with respect to reimbursable CSC 
21 


--- Page 72 ---
Case 19-G-0309 
 
Rebuttal Testimony of the Revenue Requirements Panel 
 
Page 70 of 71 
costs have been unreasonable in the past.  As noted above, the primary cause 
1 
of the increase in NYC’s accounts receivable balance with respect to 
2 
reimbursable CSC costs is the fact that NYC significantly increased its 
3 
municipal work plan in 2017; not due to a delay in billing as the SRRP 
4 
asserts. The Company reflects the billings to NYC after the work is 
5 
completed and the project is closed to plant in service.  When billing is 
6 
performed, the Company credits the funding project and the balance is 
7 
included in non-interest bearing CWIP. As a result, the gross capital 
8 
investment closed to plant in service and the billing credit in non-interest 
9 
bearing CWIP are both included in rate base resulting in a net capital 
10 
investment and no change in earnings base.  Additionally, AFUDC ceases 
11 
to be accrued once the project is in service and closed to plant.  The timing 
12 
of when the Company bills the work and receives the reimbursement from 
13 
NYC does not impact the earnings base or AFUDC.  The billing adjusts 
14 
plant; however, there is an equal and offsetting EBcap adjustment leaving 
15 
earnings base unchanged.  Therefore, the SRRP’s assertion that plant could 
16 
be overstated is incorrect. 
17 
 
 
18 


--- Page 73 ---
Case 19-G-0309 
 
Rebuttal Testimony of the Revenue Requirements Panel 
 
Page 71 of 71 
IX. 
Flow-Through Adjustments 
1 
Q. 
Has the Company reflected any additional flow-through adjustments 
2 
in its revenue requirement? 
3 
A. 
Yes.  Changes to O&M expense have an impact on working capital 
4 
requirements.  Additionally, any change in rate base results in a change to 
5 
return on rate base.  Moreover, there are flow-through adjustments for 
6 
productivity, payroll taxes, uncollectible expense, income taxes, and 
7 
deferred taxes.  The flow-through adjustments are reflected in Exhibit ___ 
8 
(RRP-1R).

SUBJECT:
Other Expenses- KEDNY 
Request: 
Note: In all interrogatories, all requests for workpapers or supporting calculations shall be 
construed as requesting any Word, Excel or other computer spreadsheet models in 
original electronic format with all formulae intact and unlocked. 
Referring to the two attached documents “DPS-548 Attachment 1 – KEDNY Other Expense by 
Activity Description,” and “DPS-548 Attachment 2 – KEDNY Other Expense by Vendor,” 
which are pivot tables created by Staff using the Company provided excel files detailing the 
KEDNY historic test year costs of $33.078 million as shown on Exhibit__(RRP-3), Summary, p. 
1 and the MS Excel File Exhibit__(RRP-11), Workpapers to Exhibit___(RRP-3), Schedule 7, 
WP 1 for the O&M cost element “Other expense.”  The first schedule is a summary of historic 
test year costs identified by activity description, and the second schedule provides the historic 
test year costs broken down by vendor. 
The activity description of Maint Svcs-Repair Gas Leak Maint includes $3.541 million of 
charges paid to the vendor titled “Finance Commissioner.”  Explain in detail what type of costs 
these charges represent. 
Response: 
 
These costs represent summonses associated with Notice of Violations (“NOVs”) of permit 
conditions issued by the New York City Department of Transportation (“DOT”). 
To construct, operate, and maintain KEDNY’s natural gas distribution system, the Company 
must regularly excavate in streets and sidewalks in the City of New York.  For all non-
emergency work, the Company secures a street opening permit in advance from the DOT.  The 
Company electronically submits a permit application, that is reviewed by the DOT, and a permit 
is issued.  The permit indicates, among other items, the area to be excavated and any work 
restrictions.  From time to time, the Company receives NOVs in connection with these street 
Exhibit ____ (RRP-4R) 
Page 1 of 46


--- Page 103 ---
Form 103 
openings.  These violations include failure to follow permit conditions such as work hour 
restrictions, working outside of the permit area, and failure to restore the area to the DOT’s 
standards.  Many of these violations are the result of unanticipated field conditions (e.g., 
subsurface facilities, parking conditions, heavy traffic) that necessitate work beyond the scope of 
the permit.  KEDNY is focused on reducing the number of violations related to street opening 
permits and traffic violations.  The Company reviews and analyzes NOVs to identify root causes 
and review work practices, implement process and reporting enhancements to increase awareness 
and improve performance, and inform whether additional training is required to reduce NOVs.   
Attachment 1 is a report summarizing KEDNY’s efforts to reduce the number of municipal 
violations incurred in connection with the Company’s road openings, street closing, and related 
activities for year ended December 31, 2018.

Roadwork and Traffic Violations Report for Year Ended 2018 
  
 
 The Brooklyn Union Gas Company d/b/a National Grid (“KEDNY” or “Company”) 
submits this report describing its efforts to reduce the number of (i) municipal violations incurred 
in connection with the Company’s road openings, street closing, and related activities and (ii) 
traffic violations, for the year ended December 31, 2018 pursuant to the Joint Proposal adopted by 
the Public Service Commission’s (the “Commission”) Order Adopting Terms of Rate Plans and 
Establishing Gas Rate Plans (Issued and Effective December 16, 2016) in Case 16-G-0059.1   
 
I.  BACKGROUND 
KEDNY provides natural gas service to 1.2 million customers in the Boroughs of 
Brooklyn, Queens, and Staten Island in the City of New York.  KEDNY operates a gas distribution 
network comprised of more than 4,100 miles of underground gas pipelines.  To construct, operate, 
and maintain KEDNY’s natural gas distribution system, the Company must regularly excavate in 
streets and sidewalks in the City of New York.  For all non-emergency work, the Company secures 
a street opening permit in advance from the New York City Department of Transportation 
(“DOT”).  The Company electronically submits a permit application, which is reviewed by the 
                                                 
1 The Joint Proposal requires a report describing KEDNY’s efforts to reduce notice of violations within 90 days after 
the close of each Rate Year, the twelve months ending December 31 of each year, of KEDNY’s rate plan, beginning 
with Rate Year One (January 1, 2017 through December 31, 2017). 
The Brooklyn Union Gas Company 
d/b/a National Grid NY
Case 19-G-0309/0310
Attachment 1 to DPS-550
Page 3 of 13
Exhibit ____ (RRP-4R) 
Page 5 of 46


--- Page 107 ---
 
 
3 
NYC DOT and a permit is issued.  The permit indicates, among other items, the area to be 
excavated and any work restrictions.   
From time to time, the Company receives violations (“Notice of Violations” or “NOVs”) 
in connection with these street openings. These violations include failure to follow permit 
conditions, work hour restrictions, working outside of the permit area, and failure to restore the 
area to the DOT’s standards.  Many of these violations are the result of unanticipated field 
conditions (e.g., subsurface facilities, parking conditions, heavy traffic) that necessitate work 
beyond the scope of the permit.  For example, the Company may be required to work in a larger 
area or for a longer period than was contemplated at the time the permit was secured because of 
the conditions observed when the underground facilities are exposed.  To complete the job, 
minimize disruptions, and ensure the provision of safe and reliable gas service to customers, the 
Company may be required to perform work that may not strictly conform to the permit stipulations 
in these cases.  The Company may also work beyond the permit scope to expedite construction 
activities to accommodate local parking, traffic flow on critical roadways, or local business needs. 
The Company works with the NYC DOT to amend street opening permits, when possible.  
However, changes to the scope/duration of street work that are identified in real time or during off 
hours, do not always afford the opportunity to secure permit modifications.  The Company’s efforts 
to coordinate permit modifications have been further challenged by a steady increase in the number 
of permits required in connection with KEDNY’s increased construction activity.  In 2014, the 
Company applied for approximately 20,000 street opening permits in NYC (including KEDLI’s 
territory in the Rockaways); approximately 35,000 in 2015 and 2016; approximately 40,000 in 
2017; and approximately 66,000 in 2018 – a more than 200 percent increase in just four years. 
The Brooklyn Union Gas Company 
d/b/a National Grid NY
Case 19-G-0309/0310

KEDNY also operates a fleet of more than one thousand vehicles to support field 
operations.  The nature of the Company’s business necessitates over five million driving miles 
each year across the Company’s service territory.  KEDNY prioritizes the safe operation of its 
vehicles, and its drivers undergo comprehensive driver safety training.  However, from time to 
time, the Company’s drivers/vehicles are issued citations for various moving and non-moving 
violations.    
KEDNY is focused on reducing the number of violations related to street opening permits 
and traffic violations.  As discussed in this report, the Company is reviewing and analyzing NOVs 
to identify root causes and review work practices, implementing process and reporting 
enhancements to increase awareness and improve performance, and providing additional training 
to reduce NOVs.    
 
II. 
 CONTINUED EFFORTS TO REDUCE ROADWORK VIOLATIONS 
Beginning in 2017, internal resources (over 40 individuals) from various business functions 
within the Company participated in numerous process improvement workshops to identify internal 
and external root causes contributing to NYC DOT violations incurred by KEDNY.   This effort 
identified both internal and external factors and root causes contributing to DOT violations.  The 
following summarizes the continuing effort, accomplishments, and impact of the process 
improvements for calendar year 2018. 
A.  Transition to NYCSTREETS.NET 
In June 2018, the DOT required KEDNY transition to the DOT’s NYCSTREETS.NET 
web-based platform for ordering, reissuing, and renewing all permits by December 2018.  The team 
currently tasked with reducing summonses supported the Company’s transition from its internal 
The Brooklyn Union Gas Company 
d/b/a National Grid NY
Case 19-G-0309/0310
Attachment 1 to DPS-550
Page 5 of 13
Exhibit ____ (RRP-4R) 
Page 7 of 46


--- Page 109 ---
 
 
5 
permit management system to NYCSTREETS.NET.  While the transition was successfully 
completed as required, it affected efforts for reducing overall summonses due to the shift of the 
team’s duties. 
Despite a short-term impact to progress on reducing overall summonses, the Company 
expects the transition to NYCSTREETS.NET and future enhancements of the platform by DOT 
will allow KEDNY to more effectively manage its permits in line with the DOT’s expectations.  
Currently, a nightly data output allows the Company to account for and report on all permits 
ordered – a feature not present prior to the transition.  Additionally, KEDNY received permission 
from the DOT to explore automation opportunities to order, renew, and reissue roadwork permits, 
a process improvement implemented prior to the transition, on the new web-based platform. 
B.  Process Automation 
KEDNY is currently utilizing Robotics Process Automation technology to renew and 
reissue a select group of both expired and upcoming expiring permits.  The Company is looking 
to expand this technology to all its internal business units, expanding the concept to all permit 
reissues and renewals as well as new permit orders.  By doing this, the Company expects to see a 
reduction in “No Permit” related summonses by removing the manual effort needed to manage 
them entirely.  In calendar year 2018, the Company targeted paving related summonses, also 
known as D4F – Overdue for Final Restoration summonses.  The Company saw a 24% reduction 
in these violations incurred compared with the prior year due to Robotics Process Automation, as 
shown in the chart below.

C.  Collaboration with DOT and External Contractors 
In addition to automation, the Company set up monthly meetings with the DOT to discuss 
specific issues related to NOVs.  This collaboration has opened avenues for future improvements 
to be made to reduce summonses incurred.  Monthly process improvement meetings have been 
established with the Company’s major contractors as well to ensure that both parties are working 
together to resolve field related issues resulting in NOVs. 
D.  Internal Process Improvements 
The following are internal process improvements implemented in calendar year 2018: 
• The Company recently established a system improvement to create internal 
transparency for sending and receiving up-to-date information for barricade and plate 
pick-ups.  Trucks receive orders for material pick-up upon job completion.  The 
Company is also exploring route optimization opportunities to further increase its 
efficiencies with material pickup.

• Engineers in charge of designing and managing projects in the field are now routinely

• The Company has streamlined its summons response process by funneling summons

responsibility. 
• The Company has added a designated supervisor to manage the Corrective Action 
Request End-to-End process and have daily communication with contractors 
addressing those job sites.  
• The Company has improved its NOVs reporting with monthly reports to leadership on 
NOVs incurred by internal function down to the project manager / engineer level.  
Additionally, these reports show the top violations incurred by function to facilitate 
identification of areas of improvement.  This increased transparency allows leadership 
to take necessary actions to improve performance across their functional teams.

E.  Changes to DOT Violation Enforcement 
Overall, summonses have increased approximately $2 million over CY17.  This is largely 
attributed to an observed 100+% increase in stipulation violations incurred ($2.2 million increase 
over last year) due to the DOT enforcement of stipulation violation codes 078 and 016.  Both codes 
pertain to the clearing of the roadway and sidewalk at the end of each work day.  These violations 
were only selectively enforced in years prior but are now being enforced on every job site where 
a permit for roadwork exists.  If the enforcement continues at this rate, the Company will need to 
determine how best to handle as the cost and effort to remove all materials and barricades at the 
end of each day could be significant.  The Company is currently in discussion with DOT officials 
regarding this concern.  Notably, without this change in enforcement practices, the Company 
would have seen a 1% decrease in stipulation violations CY18 over CY17: 
Total NOVs Incurred CY2018 Comparison ADJUSTED (Assuming no Stipulation

F.  Total NOVs Normalized based upon Volume of Permits Ordered 
The Company’s portfolio of construction activity has steadily grown over the recent years 
to meet the needs of customers.  This translates to increased exposure for potential NOVs based 
upon the sheer volume of permits required to perform this work.  In 2014, the Company applied 
for approximately 20,000 street opening permits in NYC; approximately 35,000 in 2015 and 2016; 
approximately 40,000 in 2017; and approximately 66,000 in 2018.  This reflects a 230% increase 
in just four years.  The below chart demonstrates a normalized data reduction in summonses of 
approximately 40% in calendar year 2018 compared to calendar year 2017 if the volume of work 
had not increased.

and camera violations (i.e., violations for, among others, speeding in a school zone, driving in a 
bus lane, and failure to stop at a red light).  Since creating process improvements and enhanced

As a result of an analysis of tickets received in 2016, KEDNY determined that 37 percent 
of the tickets were due to failure to display a muni-meter receipt.  After several process workshops, 
KEDNY identified an opportunity for tickets to be dismissed by having operators submit copies 
of expired muni meter receipts.  As part of a pilot, the Company equipped operators with muni-
meter prepaid cards to purchase muni meter receipts and avoid tickets.  As a result of the pilot, 
tickets received due to failure to display muni meter receipt decreased by 18%.  In 2018, the 
Company rolled out a muni meter app out to all employees to further decrease costs from tickets 
received from muni meters with no receipt.  
In addition to reporting and analysis, the Company continued a standard process change 
from 2016 to expedite payment of violations received by replacing once a month check payments 
with payments weekly via credit card for camera violations and bi-weekly via ACH transfer for

The Company also continue to utilize the DriveCam Program to improve driver behavior, 
including risky behavior resulting in parking and traffic violations. The DriveCam Program 
enables daily tracking of violations and reporting of events including collision, near collision 
avoidance, driver conduct, traffic violations, driver awareness, distractedness, and seatbelt use.  
The Program provides training and, presentations on driver behaviors for lessons learned, and 
identification and assessment of coaching needs and effectiveness, risk scores and trends, and 
repeat offender status by team and individual.  Data from DriveCams for the evaluation of driver 
performance together with the training and coaching from the DriveCam Program are intended to 
identify and improve driver behavior, including risk behavior that often results in parking and 
traffic violations. 
 
IV. CONCLUSION 
KEDNY is committed to reducing the number of traffic violations and violations related 
to street opening permits.  As discussed in this report, the Company has undertaken, and continues 
to undertake, significant efforts to reduce the number of violations for the benefit of customers and 
the public.
